### Page 1

#inflation #australia #rba #economicbulletinsaustralia #inflation
August CPI – Came in on our expectations
By Justin Smirk & Neha Sharma
15:27 September 30 2026 Share
August Monthly CPI: Headline 0.4%mth/4.0%yr, Trimmed Mean 0.2%mth/3.6%yr. Market
services ex volatile –0.4%mth/3.3%yr.

The CPI rose 0.4% in August, spot on Westpac’s expectation but a bit softer than market
expectations of 0.5%. Due to base effects the annual pace picked up to 4.0%yr from 3.5%yr.

### Page 2

Key factors driving the headline result were auto fuels (14.8%); games, toys & hobbies (10.2%) with
rents providing a solid base (0.3%) while some offset was provided by both domestic (–4.9%) and
international holiday travel & accommodation (–1.5%).
Trimmed mean (TM) inflation printed 0.2% again bang on Westpac’s expectation while the market
was slightly higher at 0.3%. The annual pace was flat at 3.6%yr, the third month in a row at this pace,
while the six-month annualise pace was steady at 3.9%yr.

Key factors driving the TM print were: rents (0.3%) and new dwelling purchases (0.2%). The TM
distribution ranged from –0.38% to 0.82%.
Today’s inflation update revealed price discounting for some durable goods as retail demand softens.
We would expect to see a bounce from these categories in the following month. There was also
offsetting strength elsewhere in durable good so at least for now, the momentum continues to
suggest some near term upside risk to our current inflation estimates.

Nevertheless, as the TM came in on expectations and based on a first quick pass at updating the
quarterly profile, we see little pressure to revise our current Q3 estimates of 1.3%qtr for the CPI and
0.9%qtr for the TM.
The RBA remains alert to near term inflationary risks. We are expecting the momentum in TM
inflation to remain well above the top of the target band through to year-end underpinning our
expectations for the RBA to raise rates again in November.

### Page 3

August CPI in more detail
Following the bumper read in July, the monthly CPI moderated in August rising 0.4%mth. This was in
line with our expectations and slightly softer than the market consensus. This took the year-ended pace
to 4.0%yr. On a seasonally adjusted basis the CPI rose 0.7%mth highlighting the impact of seasonal
sales in August.

They key drivers behind the monthly lift were automotive fuel, up 14.8%mth, contributing 0.5ppts to the
headline figure. The surge resulted from the ending of the fuel excise discount on August 3 and rising
global energy prices. With crude oil prices still elevated due to the conflict in the middle east, and
growing shortages of refined products due to both the middle east conflict and Ukrainian assaults on
Russian refining capacity, auto fuel is expected to remain an inflationary impulse at least until the end
of 2026.

The monthly TM also softened, rising 0.2%mth compared to the 0.5%mth read from July. The result
was again in line with our expectations, and very close at two decimal places (0.24%mth vs
0.23%mth), but softer than the market's view. The year-ended pace held at 3.6%yr for the third
consecutive month, although it continued to ease marginally at the second decimal place. In contrast,
the six-month annualised pace has continued to trend higher since April and is currently running at
3.9%yr.

### Page 4

There is a possibility that as the ABS continues to grapple with seasonal adjustment issues we will see
revisions to the TM estimates. Last July also saw a strong 0.48%mth read which eased to 0.29%mth in
August. This is not too dissimilar from the 0.48%mth and 0.24%mth reads for 2026.

We are expecting core inflation to start to moderate as we get to year end but our current estimate for
the month of December still has the TM running at a 3.3% in the year while the six-month annualised
pace is still a chunky 3.1%. Applying a trim on the seasonally adjusted 3m/3m expenditure classes
yields a TM of 0.9%qtr currently. This is in line with our current Q3 TM estimate.

Other measures of underlying inflation told a similar story, easing on a monthly basis but holding steady
on a year-ended basis. Market services ex volatiles fell –0.4%mth to be 3.3%yr higher. Excluding both
volatiles & holidays saw a 0.2%mth/4.1%yr lift.

Most market services components eased in the month (including on a seasonally adjusted basis) but
annual growth across this group as a whole remained above the RBA's 2-3% target band. The near-
term momentum (three-month annualised) is also quite strong across several components of services.
Of note include deposit and loan facilities (11.2%mth), sports participation (10.9%), clothing repair
(7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other household services (5.2%). We are closely watching market
services as this is where inflationary risks, outside of energy, appear to be most persistent.

Due to its sizable weight in the CPI, and the fact price changes here tend to be less volatile than many
other categories, so they almost always appear in the TM estimates, housing prices remain a key
focus. Rents rose 0.3%mth in August, in line with our expectations, to be up 3.6%yr. Since April, the
seasonally adjusted rents series has been running lower than the non-seasonally adjusted series. We
are expecting rents to hold around this pace till year end before moderating modestly as we move
through 2027.

New dwellings inflation was 0.2%mth in August (softer than our expectation for a 0.4%mth rise). Since
peaking in May, monthly price growth has slowed in each successive month, potentially reflecting
increasing difficulty for developers in passing through higher costs amid softer conditions in established
housing markets and weaker demand in the face of higher interest rates. The annual pace looks to
have topped out. It was at 5.8%yr in June easing to 5.4%yr currently with the three-month annualised
pace at 3.7%. We expect dwelling price inflation to continue easing through to the end of 2027 as
falling established house prices limit the ability of builders to pass on rising construction costs. There is
a medium-term risk that as conditions improve builders may raise prices more aggressively to claw
back margins.

### Page 5

Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated in August. The share of the basket running above 3%yr
eased from 45% to 42%, with the share below 2%yr increasing from 38% to 41%.

### Page 6

Key surprises in the month:
Upside surprises

Downside surprises
Insurance surprised to the upside (0.7%mth vs 0.3%mth) after a run of weaker-than-usual reads over
the past two months.
Motor vehicle repair costs rose a solid 0.8% against our 0.3%mth expectations, with spare parts and
accessories also up 0.3%mth vs –0.4%mth.
Footwear rose 1.7%mth against our expectations for a -2.9%mth decline, the gains were broad-
based across women, men & children.
Household goods surprised to the upside. Household appliances, utensils and tools rose 1.9%mth
against our flat expectations, meanwhile household textiles fell –1.0%mth against our expectations
for a –5.0%mth fall. Furniture and furnishings also fell by less than expected –0.6%mth vs –1.3%mth.
Recreation and culture surprised to the upside (–1.0%mth vs –1.5%mth), owing to a 1.4%mth lift in
other recreation, sport & culture against our expectations for a flat read on the back of an outsized
gain in games, toys & hobbies (10.2%mth vs 2.5%mth). Holiday travel was marginally less weak than
expected (–3.3% vs –3.5%mth).

### Page 7

Food was the key downside miss falling –0.1%mth against our expectations for a 0.2%mth lift. Meat
& seafood (–0.9%mth vs 0.5%mth) and fruit & vegetables (–1.2%mth vs 0.1%mth) were the key
areas of weakness.
Audio, visual and computing equipment and services came in weaker-than-expected (0.7%mth vs
1.2%mth), owing to a softer 0.6%mth lift in computing services (3.0%mth expected). This was partly
balanced by a stronger than expected lift in computing equipment (0.9%mth vs –0.5%mth). The latter

is where we are most likely to see the impact of the AI boom in the CPI, due to computer chip
inflation, other than construction cost inflation for new dwellings.
Motor vehicles came in lower at –0.4%mth vs –0.1%mth which could be the start of price pressures
associated with the rapid expansion of the share of Chinese cars in the Australian market.
Automotive fuel was also slightly softer at 14.8%mth vs 16.7%mth.
Garments and accessories were both softer. Garments fell –1.9%mth against our expectations for a

0.2%mth rise, meanwhile accessories came in at 0.1%mth vs 0.5%mth expected.

### Page 8

What does this mean for the RBA?
Yesterday, post the RBA rate hike and monetary policy decision statement, Westpac changed its view
from a November follow up rate hike being a risk to being the base case. The post-meeting statement
was notable for its emphasis on the Middle East conflict, noting that “global energy prices are now
much higher than had been assumed in the August forecasts”. In addition, RBA’s liaison suggests firms
are passing this on or planning to do so. The pass-through was characterised as partial, and additional

to domestically driven inflation arising from capacity pressures. We read this as implying that the RBA
is concerned about upside risks to underlying inflation in Q4 of this year, as well as Q3.

### Page 9

Related articles
Our current forecast of 0.9%qtr for the September quarter TM stands post today’s release and
represents a step up from 0.8% in the June quarter holding the annual pace at 3.6%yr. Our current
forecast for the Q4 TM is 0.7%qtr/3.3%yr but given the move in energy prices, and anecdotes of firms
now passing on those rising costs, we too see upside risks to this estimate.

As such, we hold to the view that the RBA will follow up with another rate hike in November. For more
information on the RBA decision please see Luci Ellis’s note “RBA hikes in September, November
follow-up now expected".

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• The recent tendency of some firms to bundle into prices
card-acceptance costs that had previously been surcharged
– and then some, in some cases – demonstrates a ‘cost-
plus’ approach to pricing. To the extent they can pass on
these costs through to prices, it suggests that demand was

strong enough to bear it. The RBA will take this as a signal
that demand is still outstripping supply.
• This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. Base wages
growth has been moderate for some years, though the
above-average increase in award wages this year will add
a little. Not all firms will seek to pass these cost increases
on, but some will, including because they have limited
scope to absorb the cost increase into margins.
• A cost-plus mentality has drawbacks, including that it

limits the imagination away from finding alternative,
efficiency-improving responses. It also accords with
recent academic research suggesting that firms form
expectations and set prices according to what is
happening in their own industry and experience, while
putting little weight on economy-wide inflation or central
bank credibility. This is an unpleasant message for many
consumers or anyone in an interest-sensitive sector when
supply shocks abound.

The recent banning of card surcharges has caused
considerable upset this month, even though it is a reversion
to historical and international practice. The card schemes
used to impose “no-surcharge” rules, until the RBA banned
these in 2003, and many other countries still have them. The
turnaround represents the RBA throwing in the towel on its
longstanding effort to use price incentives to get people to
choose low-cost payment methods. Surcharges no longer

made sense in a largely cashless economy. In any case, least-
cost routing puts the power to choose lower-cost methods in
the hands of the merchants who actually bear the cost.
In most cases, firms will simply roll the surcharge into the sticker
price, leaving the total cost to the consumer unchanged, just
more transparent. We also understand that some large retailers
have absorbed the cost – if they were surcharging in the first
place, which many did not. (The RBA estimated that around

16% of businesses imposed surcharges.) Inevitably, though,
some firms have lifted their prices by more than any reasonable
estimate of the merchant service fee that the surcharge was
meant to cover. And because this is Australia, of course takeaway
coffee has been the centre of consumer discontent.
Higher prices for one’s caffeine fix was unlikely to be the main
instigator of this month’s slump in consumer sentiment. Petrol
‘Cost-Plus’ costs
Forecast changes

• A full update to our forecasts was published in our October
Market Outlook  on Thursday 15 October.
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
prices are typically more salient for cost-of-living concerns, and
the before-and-after picture on the RBA announcement that week
shows that higher interest rates also contributed to the decline.
In some senses, though, the post-surcharge prices and the
RBA’s decisions are linked: businesses passing on increased

costs into their own prices – and expecting to be able to
make that increase stick – is exactly the kind of inflationary
environment the RBA is leaning against. Firms have been
subjected to an array of non-labour cost escalations, from
pandemic-era supply chain issues to electricity to fuel to
water to building supplies. And IT equipment prices, which
for decades were a reliable source of cost reductions, are
nowadays flat to up. Passing these costs on, leaving margins

intact, demonstrates that demand is not weak enough to
prevent this, and signals that firms’ inflationary expectations
might be higher than the RBA desires. The RBA will take this
as a sign that demand is still outstripping supply.
This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. While the Fair
Work Commission did hand down an above-average increase in
award wages this year and this was probably passed through
to prices in hospitality and other labour-intensive sectors, base

wages growth has been moderate for some years. Business
surveys point to non-labour cost escalation being the real issue
lately. This is why we were puzzled by the passage in the RBA
minutes this week suggesting that cost pressures reported
in business surveys might imply that the unemployment rate
consistent with inflation at target (the NAIRU) has risen. It is
a convenient belief, though, if unemployment is starting to
overshoot your forecasts, as the latest labour market data have

done compared with the RBA’s August forecasts.
Not all firms can pass on cost increases. Most small firms do
not have the market power, though coffee may be an exception
because convenience induces a kind of local market power. And
as noted above, large firms with everyday-low-price strategies
will instead try to find savings elsewhere in the supply chain.
Still, some firms will seek to pass these cost increases on.
Some observers might see this as corporate greed, but another

way to frame this strategy is simply as a ‘cost-plus’ mentality.
“Entrenched expectations that cost increases
can and should be passed on are precisely
what tight monetary policy is leaning against,
but only indirectly."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
It turns out that cost-plus pricing is especially common in
areas where regulators determine the price – water and other
utilities being examples where costs and prices have escalated
a lot in recent years.
There are limits to firms’ ability to absorb costs, too. The

failures of so many building firms a few years ago, squeezed
between post-pandemic cost escalation, delivery delays and
fixed-price contracts, were a case in point. Normally, there is
a contractual response, and it need not end in insolvency. The
episode does illustrate, however, that it is unrealistic to expect
firms never to pass cost increases through to prices.
The cost-plus mentality does have its drawbacks. It limits
the imagination away from finding responses that might save

costs elsewhere to absorb the cost hike, and in so doing, boost
efficiency. It was equally limiting in times of soft demand: in
the years before the pandemic, many firms professed a belief
that, because they couldn’t get price increases to stick, they
could not possibly accept cost increases, including in wages.
Thus, Australia ended up with wages growth too low to be
consistent with inflation at target, and the peculiar spectacle
of a central bank governor complaining that wages growth

was too low. And if labour remains cheap, why invest to
economise on it and thereby boost productivity?
A cost-plus mentality also amplifies the pass-through of
supply shocks. This is not quite the same thing as the ‘inflation
expectations’ seen in standard economic models of inflation,
but it is related. The stronger the tendency towards cost-plus
pricing, the worse the inflation response to a supply shock.
This is also true when inflation expectations are high, but the

distinction matters. The recent academic literature includes
some fascinating empirical evidence  from New Zealand that
suggests that beliefs about current and future overall inflation
have little bearing on many firms’ pricing decisions. As related
work using French data shows, firms’ pricing choices are
shaped less by economy-wide inflation developments than by
what is happening to their costs and in their industry.
That adds up to economy-wide behaviour that looks a lot

like firms (in aggregate) setting prices according to expected
inflation (in aggregate), but under the hood something
quite different is going on. If firms’ beliefs about future
economy-wide inflation do not matter much in their pricing
decisions, then neither does the credibility of a central bank’s
commitment to return inflation to target. The mechanism to
get inflation down then must be simply to weaken demand
enough to convince firms that cost-plus will not fly in the

market. This is an unpleasant message for many consumers
or anyone in an interest-sensitive sector when supply shocks
abound. It does, however, imply that if supply shocks fade
or even self-correct, so will high inflation. If and when that
happens, tight monetary policy can be unwound.
The deeper question is whether cost-plus price dynamics can
be reduced. That is more likely to be a question for regulators
than for central banks.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
bias towards an October or December decision. Williams
stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in
this cycle given known information. And Waller also showed
confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are

doing some of the FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest
rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating that
one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation
near the 2.0%yr medium-term target. However, as discussed
in this month’s Market Outlook , capacity pressures are likely
to see inflation consistently above target, rather than below,
in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity in the
housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to

remain modestly restrictive over the forecast horizon and will
also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade
account. At USD133.5bn, the September result was a whisker
inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More
impressively, throughout 2025 and 2026 year-to-date the
surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the
average of 2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth
of export growth is as impressive as the headline annual gain of

36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese
investment in neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time,
both trade and income receipts from offshore investment should
buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound
foreign investment should moderate the pace of gains against
other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the
evolution of producer prices across Asia, and the likelihood

of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent
decades continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was
one of the key drivers of declining goods prices. While
still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing
development to instead be driven by capital, technology and
energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking
production are also increasingly prioritising resilience, security
and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore more

probably future supply-chain developments will help contain
rising costs through productivity than drive sustained price
declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September
Labour Force Survey . Employment surprised to the
downside, –39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force
participation from August’s near-historic high largely offset.
The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August

was revised up.
Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless,
the take home point from the update is that the labour
market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs
below working-age population growth. We expect the
unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter
financial conditions and cost-of-living pressures restrict
activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast
peak is near at 4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the

communication challenge for the RBA. The September minutes
were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing
on materialising upside risks to inflation. But, with the
unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption
of 4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the
labour market is becoming harder to defend. Still, with a clear
focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our
near-term CPI forecasts in the October Market Outlook  – we

still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in
September, taking the annual rate up to 3.7%yr. Energy prices
were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation
was a touch above consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr).
Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months
(0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4% (3.1%yr) on a

0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport
services and a 0.3% increase in medical care services (2.7%yr
and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release
of the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures,
with the headline and core measures up 6.3%yr and 5.0%yr
respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these
developments, firms across multiple districts citing tariffs,
insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost

pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect
further price increases as contracts/hedges roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is
enough signal in these reports to warrant a follow-up hike
at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee
members believe they are near the end of the tightening cycle
and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman
both showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Downward revisions to 2027 outlook
This week we have updated our New Zealand economic
outlook for the next year, in particular reflecting the latest
developments in the energy market and key financial
variables (long-term interest rates and the exchange rate).

After last week’s marking down of our forecast for through-
year GDP growth in 2026 to 2.5%yr, this week we have
reduced our forecast of growth in 2027 by 0.3ppts to 2.7%yr.
This downward revision reflects a less positive assessment
of the outlooks for consumption and investment (including
residential investment) considering the more elevated path
assumed for energy prices and a higher near-term track for
long-term interest rates (as discussed in our updated Market

Outlook, published earlier this week).
A partial offset to weaker growth in domestic demand is
provided by a slightly more positive outlook for exports
(particularly exports of manufactured goods and tourism
inflows) because of a downward revision to our expectations
for the NZ dollar. We now expect NZD/USD to end this
year around current levels at 0.56 – 3c lower than expected
previously – before appreciating to around 0.64 over the
course of 2027 as interest rate differentials with the US

gradually close and as the USD appreciates. We think that the
low value of the NZ dollar – in trade-weighted terms now at a
17-year low – helps to explain the recent strong performance
in the previously moribund manufacturing sector, as well as
tourist inflows from key markets such as Australia.
Last week we raised our forecast for CPI inflation at year end
to 4.3%yr, largely on account of recent developments in global
energy prices (although the weak NZ dollar has compounded the

effect). This week we have slightly revised up our forecast for
inflation for the first half of 2027. But because energy prices are
assumed to decline in 2027 from what is now a higher starting
level, our 2027 year-end forecast for inflation has nudged
down 0.1ppts to 1.6%yr. Needless to say, forecasts of headline
inflation, in particular, are subject to significant revision given the
uncertain outlook for the Middle East conflict and uncertainty

about how that will be reflected in global energy prices.
Ahead of next week’s very important Q3 CPI report – which
could prove decisive – we have made no changes to our
forecast for the RBNZ’s policy settings. So, for now we
continue to think that despite the higher near-term inflation
outlook the RBNZ will stick with the strategy communicated
in the September Monetary Policy Statement, i.e. that at the
vv28 October meeting it will pause to assess the impact of this

year’s 50bps of policy tightening, before considering a further
upward adjustment in December.
Turning to this week’s data, the news from the housing market
remained decidedly downbeat. House sales fell a further 4% in
September – the seventh consecutive month of decline – to be
12% lower than a year earlier. With the stock of listings at an
11-year high, the median selling time remains elevated. And
so unsurprisingly, house prices fell for a fourth consecutive

month and are now down 1.6%yr. Prices remain especially
soft in the North Island, with prices down more than 3%yr in
Auckland and more than 4%yr in Wellington. Given upward
pressures on mortgage rates, broader strength in the economy
– including a stronger labour market – will likely be required to
reverse this trend. On that score, MBIE’s measure of online job
advertisements was little changed in September, continuing
the flat trend since the beginning of this year.

In other news, retail spending, as measured by electronic card
payments, decreased 0.4%m/m in September following an
unrevised 0.9% decline in August. This was a weaker result than
we had expected based on the recent sawtooth pattern and
our own internal data. We should treat these data with some
suspicion given that for quite a while now the Stats NZ series
seems not to have reflected the spending seen through banks’
own card spending networks, perhaps suggesting changes in the

payments arena may be leaving the official sector data behind.
Even so, unsurprisingly, with petrol prices rebounding late in
the month, spending on fuel increased 2.9%mth. However,
core spending, which excludes spending on fuel and vehicles,
fell 0.7%m/m. While spending on consumables (groceries)
increased modestly in September, this was at the expense of
spending on ‘nice to haves’. Spending on hospitality suffered a
Darren Gibbs
Senior Economist

further 2.3%mth fall following a 1.9%mth decline in August,
and spending on apparel replicated the 2.0%mth decline
reported in August. Spending on durable goods also fell
slightly in September (-0.4%mth).
Despite the weak outcome for the August and September

months, retail spending over the September quarter still
eked out a minor 0.1% gain, while core spending increased
0.4%. Given higher prices, at face value this points to a weak
quarter for volumes (next week’s CPI report will cast more
light on pricing behaviour during the quarter). It is worth
noting that total retail spending, as will be depicted in the
official quarterly retail sales survey released next month, could
point to somewhat stronger growth. Purchases of big-ticket

items that often are bought on credit – such as cars, furniture
and appliances – are underrepresented in the electronic card
spending survey.
Given subdued domestic demand, local hospitality businesses
have relied on foreign visitors to drive growth in spending.
And the good news is that visitor arrivals increased to a new
post-Covid high in August to be up 8%yr. Arrivals of Chinese
citizens have grown by almost a third to sit near pre-Covid
levels in recent months, while arrivals of US citizens grew

17%yr. However, the Middle East conflict has impacted
long-haul arrivals from Europe (UK arrivals are down 8%yr).
Meanwhile, inflows of migrants remain at modest levels,
totalling around 20,000 in the year to August – about half
what might be expected on average over the cycle.
News from the manufacturing sector remained positive in
September, with the BusinessNZ Manufacturing PMI rising
to a 3-month high of 56.5 in September. Improvement was
seen across all subcomponents of the index, with the new

orders index rising 1.8pts to 57.0. We think that the recovery in
manufacturing this year owes much to the weakness of the NZ
dollar – especially against the Australian dollar – and activity
associated with a strong domestic primary sector and an
emerging recovery in domestic residential construction activity.
Last but certainly not least, ahead of next week’s all-important
Q3 CPI, the Selected Prices report for September was firmer
than expected. That was mainly due to larger than expected

increases in airfares and holiday accommodation costs, which
offset softer than expected food prices.
Accounting for the September prices data, we’ve left our
forecast for Q3 inflation  at 0.7%. That would see the annual
inflation rate dropping back to 3.7%, down from 4.1% in the
year to June. Underlying that move has been a moderation in
fuel prices after they rocketed higher in the wake of the Middle
East war. That’s balanced against increases in council rates,

building costs and food prices.
Our forecast is lower than the RBNZ’s September MPS
forecast for a 0.8% rise (+3.9%yr). However, with some large
swings in volatile items like fuel, the RBNZ will likely focus
on measures of core inflation. Core inflation was already at
firm levels in the June quarter, with key measures sitting in
the upper part of the RBNZ’s target band. We expect that
they will take another step higher in September, to sit around

3%. Upside surprises in core inflation would be an important
development for the RBNZ ahead of the October OCR review,
as this could suggest that the lower NZD and higher oil prices
are boosting underlying price pressures by more and sooner
than they may have expected.

### Page 7

Q3 is likely to produce a very similar outcome to Q2, with 
quarterly growth of around 1.0% holding the annual rate at 
4.3%yr. Year-to-date, growth will remain around 4.7%ytd 
thanks to the Q1 result.
The detail and partial data for September will again highlight 
the weak state of the consumer and domestic-oriented 
businesses, but also the incredible strength of exports. With 
the trade surplus back at record highs, there are limits to the 
contribution possible from net exports hence, necessitating 
a strengthening of domestic demand if aggregate growth is 
to continue at the rate authorities intend into 2027. Expect 
additional stimulus through the turn of the year.

GROWTH MOMENTUM TO SUSTAIN, 
EXTERNAL RISKS LINGER

India registered  strong growth at the start of 
FY27. Sustaining this performance will depend 
increasingly on how effectively it navigates a 
more uncertain global environment. Besides 
trade fragmentation, geopolitical tensions 
leading to higher energy prices, and shifts in 
international capital flows present key external 
risks.

India joined its Southeast Asian peers in 
posting an upside surprise on growth in 
1QFY27 (Apr -Jun26), up a firm 7.8%  yoy. few 
points of note:  
A) strong 1Q growth print reinforced the view 
that India’s domestic demand cycle was more 
resilient than initially assumed, with 
consumption, public capex and manufacturing 
providing a solid base despite external shocks.  
B) prevailing tailwinds by way of indirect tax 
relief in 4Q25, real rate buffer, and easier 
monetary conditions (earlier cuts), were also 
supportive;  
C) nominal GDP growth returned above 10% 
after two years on higher deflators, though 
some segments like manufacturing faced 
negative deflators as input prices rose at a 
faster pace than output due to commodity price 
increases and import intensity;  
D) Revisions to backdated numbers, to account 
for output PPI series, pushed up FY26 growth to 
7.8% (from 7.7% earlier). Two prior years were 
also revised.

www.dbs.com/insightsdirect/macro groupresc@dbs.com 
• High frequency indicators 
might keep  2QFY growth 
close to 7%. 
• Pace to moderate in 2HFY. 
• Retail inflation is likely to 
continue ris e and broaden  in 
scope, necessitating a tighter 
policy bias.  WPI likely to r ise 
by double-digits. 
• Special swap windows helped 
to stem bearish INR 
positioning. 
• Organic capital flows need to 
catch up.  
• Liquidity management is in 
focus, with a moderate 
correlation to inflation. 
• Twin-deficit risks are partly 
offset by stronger macro 
buffers.

### Page 2

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            2

High-frequency indicators

Into July -August, high frequency 
indicators are holding up, including GST 
e-way bills, indirect tax  revenues, 
electricity demand and growth in digital 
payments, while toll collections and 
petroleum consumption slipped . For 
demand, a contained extent of increase 
in retail pump prices shielded 
households from the surge in global 
crude prices, but other market linked 
fuel variants have climbed in recent 
months. On the employment front, job 
reductions were witnessed in the 
software sector, even as fresh 
opportunities were reported in global 
capability centres , electr onics/ 
semiconductors, healthcare, auto/ 
ancillary etc.  Wage growth in services 
has improved , manufacturing has been 
steady, while farm sector moderated, 
according t o consumption surveys by 
CMIE.

Industry indicators are a mixed bag, with 
PMIs maintaining their foothold in the 
expansionary terrain, while production 
and eight core index growth come off 
highs. Auto production continues to 
expand, though output and sales might 
moderate in the next quarter on base 
effects due to indirect tax cuts in the 
same time year ago. Forward-looking 
firm surveys have improved, after 
initially coming under pressure.

Sectors associated with rural demand  
might moderate amid weaker kharif 
sowing activity and lower reservoir 
levels impacting the rabi crop activity. 
Cumulative southwest monsoon is ~15% 
below the long-term average by tail end 
of the season. This takes the country on 
course to face its driest monsoon since 
2009 (which was 18% below LPA). 
Uneven geographical spread is notable, 
with south and east India registering 
nearly 25% shortfall.  While k harif 
sowing is on track with 99% of acreage 
completed, the winter crop might come 
under pressure as weather 
developments suggest El Niño  could 
extend into 2027.

### Page 3

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            3

With nominal GDP growth to track 11-12% 
this fiscal yea r, corporate earning s 
growth is expected to fare better than 
recent few years. 1QFY27 performance 
had already set off the year on a strong 
note, barring selected sectors like O&G 
which faced sequential marginal 
pressures from operating costs. Add to 
this, the centre’s capex gr owth is in firm 
double-digits, also backed by growth in 
core imports (non-oil non-gold).

On the external sector , despite better 
performance by exports, oil and 
electronics purchases have weighed on 
the goods math. The trade deficit is set 
to stay wide amidst elevated global 
crude cost, higher industrial fuels, 
cooking gas etc,  even as a wider mix of 
suppliers have addressed energy 
shortages. Notably, the net electronics 
deficit has widened considerably, just 
as exports have improved. Likewise, core 
imports (ex oil ex gold) have stayed firm, 
which partly feeds into higher exports as 
well as broader domestic demand. 
Monthly service trade surplus has been 
rangebound around $17 -18bn in recent 
months dwarfed by $25 -$30bn goods 
gap.

Maintaining growth forecasts

High frequency indicators put 2QFY 
growth close to 7%. We expect full year 
FY27 growth to average 7.3% yoy  from 
a revised 7.8% in FY26 , assuming 
moderation in the momentum in the 
second half of the year on tighter policy 
conditions, lagged impact of high energy 
prices and base effects from indirect tax 
cuts last year fade.  Assuming some 
spillover impact of exogenous 
uncertainties and tight financial 
conditions at home, we expect FY28 
growth to average 6.8-7.0%.

INFLATION OFF ITS BACK – 
THREE CATALYSTS

After two years of benign inflation, 
India’s price trend is off its back this 
year, mainly on the back of three

### Page 4

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            4

catalysts: Food, energy and early signs 
of demand pass through.

Aug inflation rose to a 20 -month high of 
4.8% yoy from 4.5% month before . As 
the chart highlights, number of sub -
segments growing by faster than the 
inflation target at 4% have continued to 
climb, pointing to a broadening out in the 
momentum.

Food inflation is gradually becoming 
more broad -based, shifting beyond 
vegetables and pulses to sugar, milk, 
protein groups, edible oils, amongst 
others. Sub-par weather has contributed 
to this upmove, besides idiosyncratic 
factors like swing in production  trends, 
diversion for fuel usage etc. After two 
years of normal monsoon, cumulative 
southwest rainfall this year is currently 
15% below the long -term average, 
alongside slower build -up in reservoir 
levels, which could impact upcoming rabi 
crops as well.  El-Nino patterns continue 
to strengthen, adding to seasonal swings 
and risk of impacting farm output.

Beyond food, g lobal oil prices have 
averaged ~$91pb in CY26, staying above 
$100pb for much of Seo26 . Retail fuel 
prices were adjusted in quick succession 
back in May26 and have been left 
unchanged since then , while CNG, 
aviation fuel, LPG etc have  continued to 
climb.

Transport inflation jumped to 4.6% yoy in 
Aug vs 0.1% in Jan26, besides utilities’ 
component inching up. Business price 
expectation surveys (conducted by IIM 
Ahmedabad) have, nonetheless, 
captured businesses facing a notable 
increase in fuel costs, especially 
industrial fuels, base metals and 
derivative product groups. Imported 
price pressures are most notabl e in the 
producer price/ wholesale price indices. 
These gauges have been hovering at 9 -
10% yoy in recent months.

### Page 5

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            5

Lastly, core inflation  (ex-food and fuel)  
rose to 1.5-month high in August, ma inly 
due to a sequential rebound in precious 
metals – gold and silver jewellery. 
Excluding precious metals, the reading 
was more moderate at 2.9%, pointing to 
some extent of economic slack. 
Nonetheless, other pockets like telecom, 
restaurants, business services, transport 
fares etc have been inching up, 
reflecting early signs of broadening in 
price pressures (on sequential basis), 
warranting attention. These are set 
against the backdrop of strong bank 
credit growth, flush INR liquidity and 
resilience in headline growth.

Our correlation analysis between 
liquidity (as % of NDTL) and core inflation 
– see chart, points to a positive but 
modest corelation at 0.4 between the 
two variables, with an assumed year -
worth lag on inflation. The inflation -
fighting mandate will also pus h the 
central bank to reduce the surplus 
liquidity in the domestic system.

A sh arp in global commodity prices is 
expected to fe ed into the wholesale 
price gauge , pushing up reading s to 
double-digits next quarter.

A gradual broadening of price 
pressures is likely to keep headline 
inflation above 5% in second half of the 
fiscal year , sharply narrowing the real 
rate buffer, underscoring the need for a 
tighter policy bias. Add to this, recent 
developments, including a sustained rise 
in crude prices, tightening global 
financial conditions, firm domestic 
growth and signs of broadening in core 
pressures, strengthen the case for a  
shallow 50bp hike in second half of FY27, 
making October’s meeting a live one.

TACKLING LIQUIDITY DELUGE, 
HIGHER YIELDS

Beating street estimates by a wide 
margin, the RBI’s special swap windows 
raised a cumulative $ 143bn into Sep , 
with the highest contribution (~92%) by

### Page 6

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            6

FCNR(B) deposits at $1 33bn, while rest 
were via offshore borrowing facilities. 
These flows pushed up the foreign 
reserves stock to a fresh high past 
$780bn, providing considerable 
firepower to defend the currency. This is 
already noticeable in the recent shift in 
the intervention bias. USDINR, which was 
earlier stubbornly steady in the face of 
strong FCNR inflows but  eased sharply 
due to strong intervention dollar sales 
and broader dollar swings, before being 
led higher due to external triggers.

Liquidity is a key watch factor. Given the 
swap arrangement, these inflows have 
added to already abundant INR liquidity 
backdrop, which was at a four year high, 
depressing overnight rates. The banking 
system surplus jumped past INR 10trn 
surpassing 2022 and Covid highs, with 
the durable balance widening sharply 
higher to INR 14trn, suppressing 
overnight call rates. This compares to 
average INR 1trn surplus in late-June.

While organic drivers like tax -related 
outflows, and seasonal currency leakage, 
in addition to CAD, portfolio outflows, 
and maturity of the forwards book will 
act as counter -balancing factors, yet 
concerted steps were required to drain 
the potential surge in liquidity.

We discussed the various seasonal, 
near-term and permanent measures  
that the central bank might consider (see 
FCNR bonanza necessitates deft 
management) to absorb this surfeit. 
Policymakers are being mindful of both 
the liquidity and signalling effects of 
the sterilisation tools at their disposal. 
The RBI utilised tranches of market 
operations like VRRR across tenors, 
after which three tranches of open 
market operations (bonds maturing FY29 
and FY32) worth a cumulative INR 1trn 
were conducted. These announcements 
fuelled a rise in yields, which were 
already inching up, tracking global 
counterparts. Sell/buy FX swaps for  
shorter rollovers were also being 
reportedly tapped.

When the dust settles, focus will also 
be on the bunched -up maturities that 
will fall due in 3Y and 5Y tenor of the 
deposits. A portion of the existing 
reserve stock could be earmarked 
against these liabilities, helping to 
mitigate concerns that deposit 
maturities or debt repayments could 
trigger a sharp increase in dollar demand 
and exert pressure on the FX market 
down the line. In the near-term, priorities 
will be to manage liquidity, gradually 
lower the sizeable forwards book, and 
support the domestic currency. Long -
end yields continue to be influenced by 
the hardening global yields.

BALANCE OF PAYMENTS MATH 
TO BE COMFORTABLE

The FY27 current account math is 
expected to display two key counter 
trends, Firstly, the wider goods trade 
imbalance will raise the FY27 current 
account deficit. Secondly, the 
counterbalancing effect of a sharp 
improvement in the capital flows math 
will keep the annual balance of 
payments math in surplus.

### Page 7

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            7

India recorded a current account deficit 
(CAD) of $10.5bn during April-July 2026, 
compared with $6.1bn in the 
corresponding period last year. The 
deterioration was driven almost entirely 
by a wider merchandise trade deficit, 
which rose to $117.8bn from $97.1bn a 
year ago. Part of this offset by higher  
income, transfers and capital account 
flow. Capital flows found support from 
net FDI, portfolio investments and 
banking capital, especially NRI deposits 
(jumped sharply to $36bn vs $4.6bn 
same period last year) , pushing up Apr -
Jul26 BOP to $12.7bn surplus, more than 
double of $4.8bn same time last year.

Oil prices are the main swing factor for 
the CAD this year , with the current run -
rate tracking our forecast at -1.1% of GDP 
for FY27. This suggests that the 
widening in the CAD is commodity -price 
related rather than driven by higher 
demand.

On the other hand, capital flows are 
being buttressed by the special swap 
windows introduced by the central bank, 
which raised a cumulative $ 143bn 
between June and August, through non -
resident deposits and external 
borrowings. This has helped to fil l the 
gap from subdued portfolio flows and 
net investment trends. We expect FY27 
current account deficit to track -1.1% of 
GDP in FY27, with the balance of 
payments to register upwards of $80bn 
surplus (vs -$23.6bn deficit last year).  
Into FY28, the dependency of capital 
flows on o rganic drivers like portfolio 
flows and FDI is set to increase, 
underscoring the need for higher 
vigilance.

FISCAL RUN-RATE

The FY27 fiscal run -rate points to a 
stronger revenue momentum, which 
has helped to keep the FYTD deficit in 
check, even as expenditure was 
frontloaded owing to subsidy payments 
and capex disbursements. April-July 
2026 deficit stands at a third of the full-
year balance.

### Page 8

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            8

Both tax and non -tax receipts fared 
better than the comparable period year 
ago, helped by stronger corporate tax 
collections, STT transaction revenues, 
and RBI dividend, amongst others.

Setting 2QFY for a strong start, net 
direct tax collections r ose 13% yoy by 
mid-Sep26, driven by net corporate tax 
collections up 19.5%. Net non -corporate 
tax collections were up 6%, besides STT 
collections up a sharp 53% yoy. Add to 
this, divestments have also fared better 
than expected this year, with ~78% 
raised by August, accompanied by 
dividends & profits (at 83% of budget by 
Jul26).

Under expenditure, about 33% of the full 
year allocation was allocated by July 
2026. The subsidy outgo rose sharply, up 
35% yoy, led by higher food at 23% and 
fertiliser at 46% (already accounting for 
nearly two-third of the full year budget)  
led by surging global DAP and urea 
prices. With global prices remaining high, 
the actual outgo might be higher than 
budgeted INR 1.7trn , likely towards INR 
2.5trn. Capital spending also remained 
apace, up 30% yoy in first five months of 
the year.

Overall, strong growth in tax and non-tax 
receipts allowed the centre to sustain 
higher spending while containing the 
extent of fiscal slippage until July 2026 
(FYTD). A strong nominal GDP rate 
(helped by higher inflation) will also 
provide a favourable denominator effect.

IN SUM

The economy exhibited resilience in the 
face of a tough external environment in 
the first quarter, with part of that 
stability likely to extend to 2QFY27 as 
well. Tighter monetary conditions, El 
Nino impact and base effects from 
passage of post -indirect tax jump will 
moderate headline growth in 2HFY. 
Nonetheless for the year, we expected 
growth to stay north of 7%, factoring in a 
strong start.

Crude tanker rates have hit all-time highs amid the prolonged
Iran war and Strait of Hormuz disruption. Restructured oil ﬂows
and a rush for capacity to handle oil transport operating under
military protection have driven rates sharply higher, adding
further pressure to fuel prices alongside strong reﬁnery margins
Rico Luman
Record-breaking tanker rates along with operating in war zones and elevated
insurance premiums are all contributing to high fuel prices

Tanker shipping thrives in risky waters amid
a continued capacity squeeze
Oil tanker markets sit at the centre of geopolitical turmoil, and this
year that has translated into record-breaking freight rates as the
conﬂict in the Middle East drags on. Tankers have been caught up in
sanctions regimes and repeatedly targeted in key shipping lanes,
including the Strait of Hormuz, Bab al-Mandeb and the Gulf of Aden.
At the same time, demand for tanker capacity has surged as

traders seek alternative routes and sources of supply, including

Average global crude vessel earnings exceeded an unprecedented

### Page 4

Source: Clarksons, ING Research
Crude tanker orders pick up after a
prolonged downturn
Tanker markets are cyclical by nature, and there’s a clear
connection with freight rates. Over the pandemic years of 2020-21,
tanker earnings hit lows due to lower oil consumption and
shipments. This resulted in an order book of just 4% of global crude
carrier capacity, the lowest on record since the 1990s.
With the recovery of oil demand and tanker rates in 2022 and

2023, order intake began to rebound. This year, in particular, order
books have doubled from 14% to 28%. Although the average age
of the crude ﬂeet has also reached a multi-decade high of over 13
years and more scrapping can be expected, we can also expect
more supply pressure. But many of these vessels will only be
delivered in 2028-29, likely after the current boom ends. It’s all
about geopolitics and how the Middle East conﬂict evolves, but
tanker markets could still see a solid 2027.

Document 317: The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco_223e3a6a.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01

13025075     1

Economy 
The Institute Employment Report: September 2026 
07 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Job growth softened further in September, according to Bank of America's deposit data, with growth of 1.4% year-over-year 
(YoY), from 1.5% in August. But falling unemployment payments in September indicate a relatively healthy overall picture. 
• Lower-income households' wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap. After-tax wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY 
for lower-income households in September, while rising to 3.8% and 3.7% for middle- and higher-income households, 
respectively. 
• Lower tax withholdings and a relative strengthening in the younger/lower-income end of the labor market are driving this story. 
Additionally, average hours have risen in some lower-paid industries, which is also boosting pay packets.

Job growth slowed in September, but unemployment trends are benign  
Our estimate of job growth – which uses Bank of America deposit data to track changes in the number of customer accounts 
receiving a paycheck (see Methodology) – slowed further in September, to 1.4% year-over-year (YoY) from 1.5% YoY in August. 
The data can be noisy, partly due to seasonal variation and differences in pay-period timing, but in our view, the latest reading is 
consistent with the easing in payrolls growth seen in the September Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (Exhibit 1). 
Still, despite slower hiring, there is little sign of a rise in joblessness in Bank of America data. Unemployment payments into 
customer accounts showed a YoY decline in September (Exhibit 2). So, overall, labor market trends appear resilient.

After-tax wage growth cooled for lower-income, but rose for other cohorts 
Lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY in September from 4.7% YoY in August, while wage growth for middle- and 
higher-income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. Overall, these changes were modest, and lower-income 
households are continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” 
pattern we observed over much of 2025 and 2026 (Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: Lower-income wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap 
After-tax wage and salary growth by household income terciles, based on Bank of America aggregated consumer deposit account data (three-month moving 
average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In our view, the pick-up in lower- and middle-income households’ after-tax wage growth continues to reflect the impact of the 
One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBA) in reducing tax withholdings, alongside an underlying rise in job-to-job changes at the 
younger/lower-income end of the labor market (for more, read: The Institute Employment Report: August 2026). Consistent with 
that underlying pick up, BLS data shows a solid YoY rise in average hours worked in several relatively lower-paid industries, such 
as manufacturing, retail and other services. Hourly workers in these industries are likely seeing rising paychecks as a result 
(Exhibit 4).

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Estimate of payroll growth from Bank of America internal data is based on the change in customer accounts receiving a paycheck in the month. 
An adjustment is made for the difference between overall population growth and customer account growth. Occasionally historic payments can 
be reclassified, leading to revisions in the series.  
An estimate of bonus growth from Bank of America deposit data is calculated by looking at customers who have received an inbound ACH 
payroll transaction in the last two years. From this sample an estimate of bonuses is derived by looking for payroll transactions which are over 
50% higher than the median regular payroll payments received by the customer. Of these payments only those that were received around the 
same time in each of the last two years are selected.  
The job-to-job change rate (j2j rate) is defined as the proportion of customers with an identified change in their employer as a proportion of 
the total number of customers with employment income. We estimate the median pay rise associated with a j2j change using the pay in the 
first three months of the new job compared to the same three months a year ago. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS)

We expect another slight increase in the 
SGD NEER policy band slope at the MAS’ 
October review, with no change to the 
band’s width or centre. Import cost 
pressures and resilient growth favour a 
calibrated tightening, rather than a more 
forceful adjustment, in line with the 
global monetary policy stance.

Also due to be released alongside the 
MAS’ policy decision are the 3Q26 
advance GDP estimates, which we 
expect to show another quarter of strong 
growth at 5.4% yoy (1.4% qoq sa), 
compared with 5.9% yoy (1.4% qoq sa) in 
2Q26. Robust economic performance 
continued to be driven by artificial 
intelligence ( AI)-led trade activity, 
supporting the manufacturing and 
wholesale trade sectors, while strength 
in the financial sector underpinned the 
resilience of the modern services cluster. 
This came despite a moderation of the 
transport & storage sector, and 
weakness in consumer -facing food 
services and retail sectors.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Malaysia

We expect Malaysia’s advance GDP 
estimate for 3Q26 to register above -
trend growth of 5.0% yoy, albeit 
moderating from 6.0% yoy  in 2Q26. The 
overall expansion continued to be 
supported by ongoing strength in the 
export-oriented manufacturing sector, 
underpinned by strong  AI-related 
demand for electronics, and sustained 
domestic demand, backed by healthy 
investment and private consumption 
trends. However, contraction in mining 
and weak agricultural activity dragged 
overall growth.

Singapore

We expect firm Singapore non -oil 
domestic exports (NODX) growth of 28.0% 
yoy in September 2026, although 
moderating from August’s exceptional 
surge of 46.2%. Although favourable 
base effects largely faded in September, 
electronics shipments likely remained 
the primary growth driver, underpinned 
by robust AI -related demand amid 
sustained global investment in AI 
infrastructure, while non-electronics 
exports were uneven.

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 9, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

China

Exports growth is expected to remain 
robust at 25.7% y oy in September, 
supported by continued strength in 
electronics demand. Both the official and 
Caixin manufacturing PMIs rose above 
the 50 expansion threshold during the 
month, pointing to improving 
manufacturing activity. External trade 
indicators also stren gthened, with 
average daily deadweight tonnage 
handled at China's 20 major ports 
accelerating from -0.7% yoy in August to 
7.2% in September. Freight activity 
improved as well, as growth in 
international cargo flights picked up 
from -1.4% y oy to 3.6% over the same 
period.

On the price front, factory -gate and raw 
material prices rebounded amid the 
renewed escalation in geopolitical 
tensions. Main raw material purchasing 
price and producer price subPMI 
increased from 56.6 and 50.4 to 60.8 and 
54.0 during the period. However, 
downstream consumer price pressures 
are likely to remain relatively subdued 
under the pricing-band mechanism. As a 
result, CPI inflation is expected to edge 
up from 0.8% y oy in August to 1.0% in 
September. Nevertheless, soft domestic 
demand is expected to continue 
constraining the pace of consumer price 
increases.

India

September inflation is likely to gather 
momentum to 5.7% y/y, partly driven by 
base effects, from 4.8% the month 
before. Food segments continue to 
register gains, with pressures 
broadening to include perishables, 
edible oils, rice, pulses, sugar, etc., 
according to high-frequency data. Under 
non-food, retail pump prices have not 
been adjusted further, though other fuel 
products, such as non -subsidised LPG, 
diesel and ATF (still up by double digits 
y/y, though slower than in August), are 
up on the year. Core  inflation is also 
likely to tick up, aligning with the central 
bank's view that price risks are no longer 
benign. Pressures have built up due to 
the overhang of an uneven and sub -par 
monsoon, drought conditions in parts of 
the country, and elevated oil pr ices. In 
the face of higher headline prints, we 
expect the RBI to keep the door open for 
another rate hike in December 2026.

Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• Consumer spending is holding up, and we expect real PCE to rise at around a 3.0% annualized rate
in Q3.
Consumer spending has been mixed, yet resilient in recent months. After a soft July, driven largely by

weaker durable goods purchases, retail sales rebounded strongly in August, rising at a 1.2% monthly
clip amid broadly distributed gains across retailers. The latest data have led us to lift our near-term
growth forecast, and we now look for real PCE to rise at a 3.2% annualized clip in Q3. Previous data
will also likely be revised higher on firmer control group sales data and strong services revenue in Q2,
suggesting recent consumer momentum remains intact. All told, the consumer is still in decent shape

and has capacity to keep spending. Real income growth remains modest but is beginning to improve
as inflation eases and the labor market remains broadly supportive suggesting continued consumption
even if that pace of spending may moderate into year-end.
Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build-out is still king in terms of the contribution to business investment, but the
broadening in capex spending beyond the tech sector is taking root.

AI-related investment continues to dominate capital allocation. There are no signs of firms scaling
back their outlays now, though at some point as AI investment matures, merely sustaining spending
at elevated levels will provide a smaller incremental boost to growth than it has in recent quarters.
Beyond AI, the tentative signs we've identified that signal a broadening in the investment backdrop
are getting a bit louder. The ISM manufacturing index has been in expansion territory for eight-

Economics | 3

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
straight months, and durable goods orders have firmed across a wider range of industries. Part of
this improvement likely reflects delayed capital spending plans finally moving forward, but inventory
rebuilding after five consecutive quarters of drawdown may be lending some support as well.

Investment: Residential
• Residential construction looks likely to taper through the rest of the year while mortgage rates and
new home inventories remain elevated.
Headwinds have intensified against residential construction. The trend in single-family construction is
downshifting, emblematic of the affordability challenges depressing buyer demand. As of this writing,

mortgage rates have risen to over 7.0% and builder confidence has declined alongside. Sales stuck in
a low gear mean that new home listings are inching higher, further discouraging new development.
Multifamily construction appears to be holding up but faces limited potential upside with vacancy
rates elevated across much of the South. We still see a potential for marginal improvement in 2027,
but higher mortgage rates dampen our outlook.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures

• Construction appears to be gaining steam, led by data centers and power. However, sluggish
commercial starts elsewhere and high interest rates are likely to keep growth relatively modest.
The AI infrastructure build out is supporting modest growth in nonresidential construction. After
moderating for a full year, private nonresidential outlays rose from April to July. Data centers and
power are propelling current momentum. Though construction has also strengthened across sports

facilities and communication infrastructure, commercial projects remain stuck in low gear. Categories
like warehousing and retail remain soft, and overall architectural billings have improved only modestly.
Looking ahead, a recent breakout in megaprojects is likely to support additional data center and
manufacturing development, while higher interest rates limit overall growth.

Net Exports & Trade
• Net exports are tracking to slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth amid
strong imports.
Imports are shaping up for another strong quarter after a surge in July and a rebound in August
retail spending suggesting continued domestic demand for product. The July import gain was again

almost entirely driven by high-tech related goods like computers & accessories, semiconductors and
telecommunications equipment. We still anticipate a bit more of a broadening out in imports to come,
perhaps as early as August as businesses replenish inventory amid continued consumer demand.
Export growth is a bit more constrained, suggesting an expansion in the trade deficit and that net
exports will slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth in Q3.

Labor Market
• We modestly raised our 2026 payroll forecast and made no material changes to our 2027 forecast.
We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end this year at 4.2%.
August's employment report reinforced that the labor market remains stable. Payroll growth has
averaged 106K over the past six months, though we expect some moderation in the months ahead.
Hiring measures from regional Fed surveys and hiring plans from small businesses have cooled from

their early summer jumps and job openings have leveled off. At the same time, initial jobless claims
remain near multi-decade lows and continue to indicate that layoffs remain contained. We expect
payroll growth to moderate to roughly 65K per month over the remainder of the year, a pace broadly
consistent with our estimate of breakeven. As hiring cools slightly and the participation rate rebounds
somewhat further, we see some scope for the unemployment rate to edge up to 4.2% by year-end.

Even with the slight increase though, labor market conditions would remain consistent with full
employment.
Inflation
• Incorporating our expectations for the upcoming BEA methodology changes and recent inflation
data, we look for the core PCE deflator to end the year at 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis, up from
3.1% in our previous forecast.
Headline inflation remains tied to the conflict in the Middle East, although underlying price pressures
continue to move in a more favorable direction. Higher oil prices are keeping some upward pressure

on energy-sensitive categories in the near term, but tariff-related goods inflation is fading and price
pressures outside a handful of technology-related categories remain limited. Meanwhile, core services
inflation remains on a moderating trend as shelter inflation continues to gradually cool and labor cost
pressures slowly ease. Taken together, inflation pressures appear less widespread than earlier this year,
Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
but recurring supply shocks are likely to keep the path back to 2% bumpy. We expect the core PCE
deflator to slow to 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis this year.

Fiscal Policy
• We forecast this year's federal budget deficit will total $2.1 trillion (~6.5% of GDP), and next year's
will be about the same in dollar terms and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).

The federal budget deficit continues to hover in the 6.0-6.5% of GDP range, similar to where it was
at the end of the Biden administration. The outlook for the next couple of years is one of status quo.
We doubt there will be any new, big fiscal legislation with divided government likely after the midterm
elections, but rising interest costs and steady growth in mandatory spending programs such as Social
Security and Medicare will keep deficits from shrinking much. It's not just U.S. fiscal problems putting

upward pressure on long-term rates. Other factors such as robust corporate debt issuance, policy
uncertainty and foreign factors are also playing a role. Our base case for 6.0%-6.5% of GDP budget
deficits will not help when it comes to bringing down long-term interest rates.
6 | Economics

### Page 7

U.S. Economic Outlook: September 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• We have changed our fed funds forecast to 4.00-4.25% by year-end 2026, with no changes in
2027. Our year-end forecasts for the 10-year Treasury yield are also higher at 4.90% and 4.75%,
respectively, for this year and next.
The FOMC made clear at its September 16 meeting that stubbornly high PCE inflation this year was
the key factor behind the Committee's decision to raise rates. Given this, we think only a string of

good inflation data can bail the Fed out, and accordingly we think one more rate hike is coming this
year, with softer inflation data in 2027 giving way to a more patient FOMC. We could easily envision
one more hike beyond that or a "one and done" hike where the FOMC does not go again. Outcomes
outside that range, however, strike us as unlikely. A rising neutral rate makes meaningful rate cuts hard
to justify in the absence of an economic slowdown, while the case for a more aggressive tightening

cycle seems weak given that the labor market is not overheating and much of the excess inflation is
supply side driven. We think a very short gradual tightening cycle and an eventual reduction in energy
prices/slowdown in inflation will allow Treasury yields to drift modestly lower in 2027. But, the factors
mentioned above (e.g., large budget deficits, rising neutral rate) should prevent yields from falling in a
bigger way.

International Developments
• Global growth has proved more resilient than expected, prompting us to upgrade our 2026 global
GDP growth forecast to 2.9%. We have also modestly revised our global inflation outlook higher,
lifting our 2026 CPI forecast to 4.4% and our 2027 forecast accordingly.
• We now expect more tightening across advanced economies than in our previous monthly forecast
update, while emerging market policy paths remain mixed.

Global economic activity proved more resilient than expected in H1-2026, with early indicators
suggesting that momentum carried into Q3. While precautionary stockpiling supported some of the
earlier strength, activity has remained resilient on firmer services, continued AI-related investment and
exports (U.S., South Korea, Japan and Australia) and increased defense spending (Eurozone and Japan).
We expect inflation to remain higher for longer before easing next year and averaging 4% in 2027 as

tighter financial conditions weigh on demand and supply pressures fade. Risks remain skewed to the
upside. Oil and product inventories remain below pre-conflict levels, while limited refining capacity and
ongoing disruptions leave energy prices vulnerable to renewed pressure. El Niño and higher fertilizer
costs threaten food prices, while AI-related demand is raising semiconductor costs. Second-round
effects remain limited, but successive supply shocks increase the risk of persistent inflation into next

year.

AI 信贷担忧引爆去杠杆浪潮，对冲基⾦⼗个⽉内三度遭遇 " 五年⼀遇 " 极端事件。量化基⾦单

盛 Prime 经纪业务数据显⽰，量化基⾦当⽇遭受 -3.7 个标准差的重创，创下 8 ⽉ 19 ⽇以来最惨单
⽇跌幅。亚洲区对冲基⾦⽉度跌幅扩⼤⾄ -6.8% ，韩国、中国台湾 AI 相关仓位成为重灾区。⾼盛

反弹⽽收复失地。

次 " 五年⼀遇 " 事件，意味着对冲基⾦⾏业现⾏的 VaR 模型对极端⻛险的评估存在系统性低估，相
关⻛险管理框架或需从根本上重构。市场层⾯，⾼盛 Prime 账簿整体杠杆率仍⾼达 310% ，中国
台湾仓位接近五年⾼点，叠加周五期权到期⽇临近，部分正 gamma 对冲缓冲将随之消退，下⼀
轮去杠杆压⼒或将从对冲基⾦内部蔓延⾄⼤盘指数。
动量因⼦崩塌，做空轧空同步发⽣
此轮抛售的导⽕索在因⼦层⾯⼀⽬了然。

### Page 2

当多头持仓同步下跌、空头持仓遭到轧空，基⾦经理实际上并⾮在做⽅向性判断，⽽是在同步
削减多空两侧敞⼝。这⼀⾏为所 " 消耗 " 的，正是组合中最为拥挤的因⼦ —— 本轮即为动量因⼦。

⽇下跌 115 个基点，⾃ 10 ⽉ 8 ⽇本轮调整启动以来累计跌幅已达 -10% 。
量化基⾦从领跑到领跌，仅⽤⼀天

得正收益的策略，单⽇上涨 0.5% ，年初⾄今回报⾼达 21.7% ，领跑所有策略。

⽰，过去⼀年内仅有 8 ⽉ 19 ⽇⼀个交易⽇跌幅更甚。

是仓位层⾯的清洗，⽽此次抛售伴随着实质性的基本⾯疑问 ——AI 资本开⽀的融资速度与可持

续性受到质疑，这对⻛险管理者⽽⾔更难以等待穿越。
多策略基⾦⽅⾯，股票账簿（ 4.5 倍杠杆）当⽇下跌约 1.2% ，与 2 ⽉份暴跌第⼆⽇的跌幅相当。
亚洲成重灾区，韩国仓位半数已被清洗
亚洲市场是此轮抛售最惨烈的战场。
⾼盛 Prime 的 Himanshu Deshmukh 在收盘后报告中披露，亚洲基本⾯多空策略经理周四单⽇亏
损 2.6% ，⽉度回撤扩⼤⾄ -6.8% ，年初⾄今回报降⾄ 16.7% ，回到 "7 ⽉低点⽔平 "—— 距离 6 ⽉底
40% 的⾼点已下跌约 23 个百分点，今年的亮眼成绩被腰斩逾半。
抛售集中在预期⽅向：亚洲股票净卖出 -1.0 个标准差，以韩国和中国台湾为主，多头平仓与空头
回补之⽐⾼达 3 ⽐ 1 ，且韩国、中国台湾的卖盘 " 以 AI 相关多头的解仓为主 " 。⾼盛 2026 年各市场
净流向图显⽰，韩国累计净卖出约占亚洲净市值的 13% ，遥遥领先。

随着 AI 敞⼝⼤幅降低、主动净仓位偏低、利率⾛强，交易台认为存在局部超卖现象，尽管融资

Document 484: 利上げで悪化する一部AI関連企業の資金調達環境 ～半導体市場に影響も、中長期的な市場拡大は変わらず～

Source PDF: all report pdf/利上げで悪化する一部AI関連企業の資金調達環境 ～半導体市場に影響も、中長期的な市場拡大は変わらず～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

Investment Navigator

2026 年 10 月 16 日

1/4 
利上げで悪化する一部 AI 関連企業の資金調達環境 
～半導体市場に影響も、中長期的な市場拡大は変わらず～ 
 経済調査部 シニア・フェロー  嶌峰 義清 
（℡：050-5474-7489）

（要旨） 
○ FRB が9 月に3 年 2 カ月ぶりの利上げに踏み切り、 政策金利が中立金利の水準に近づくなか、
AI 関連企業の資金調達環境に変化が出てきた。 データセンターを借入で建設するネオクラウ
ド企業の社債利回りは15%近くまで上昇し、市場では信用不安の様相も見られる。 
○ 韓国の出荷・在庫バランスは、半導体、電子部品ともに悪化した。台湾でも電子・光学産業
の受注の勢いが鈍り、顧客在庫が「過剰」に転じるなど、需給が変調する兆しが出ている。 
○ それでも、 ハイパースケーラーの設備投資やメモリーの供給不足をみる限り、AI 関連需要の
拡大は中長期的に続く可能性が高い。今後は「AI ならば何でも買われる」局面から、財務の
健全性と需給をもとに銘柄が選別される局面に移ると考えられる。

1.  利上げが中立水準に届き、市場の前提が変わる 
FRBは9 月のFOMCで、政策金利の誘導目標を0.25％引き上げ、3.75～4.00％とした（図表1）。
利上げの背景にあるのは、根強いイ
ンフレだ。9 月の消費者物価指数は前
年比＋3.7％と市場予想を上回った。
市場では10 月27～28 日のFOMCで追
加利上げがあるとの見方が再び強ま
りつつある。金利の上昇は国債市場
でも顕著で、2 年債利回りは5％に迫
り、10 年債利回りも5.3％台まで上
昇している。 
FRBは、長期的に望ましい政策金利
（景気を刺激も抑制もしない中立金
利）を 3.25～3.50％とみている。足
元の政策金利はすでにその水準を超
えており、金融政策は景気を抑制す
注：色つき部分はFRBによる中立金利水準域
出所：FRB
（図表1）米国の政策金利（FF金利）の推移

（％）

### Page 2

2/4 
る領域に入っている。金融引き締めが実体経済に効いてくるまでには通常1～2 年かかる。それで
も、景気の先行きを読む金融市場は、早い段階で影響を受けやすい。 
なかでも金利上昇の影響を受けやすいのが、高い成長期待を背景に株価が形成されてきたAI・
半導体関連株だ。将来の利益への期待が大きい企業ほど、割引率が上がると理論的な株価の下落
幅が大きくなる。加えて今回は、金利上昇が株価評価の調整にとどまらず、AI投資そのものを支
える資金調達の経路にまで影響し始めた点に注意が必要だ。

2.  データセンター投資企業の社債利回りが急騰 
その象徴となったのが、AI向けクラウド事業者であるCoreWeaveの社債だ。10 月14 日、同社が
発行した2032 年満期債は14.8％程度まで上昇し、株価は1 日で15％超下落した。同日には
Oracleの株価も9％近く下落した。これを受けて、10 月 15 日の日経平均株価は前日比▲3318.34
円（▲4.9％）と、過去3 番目となる下落幅を記録した。指数への寄与が大きい半導体関連株の下
げが主導したもので、日経半導体株指数は同▲10.1％と7 月 28 日以来の下落率を記録している。 
10 月14 日のCoreWeave社の 2032 年満期債（利率6.25％）の利回りは7.24％まで上昇した。ハ
イパースケーラー（大手クラウド事業者）の大量の社債発行が続くなか、9 月下旬時点で、AI関連
の発行体の社債スプレッドは約115bp（Goldman Sachs集計）に広がった。一方、投資適格社債全
体は 83bp（ICE BofA）と、年初からほぼ横ばいにとどまっている。つまり、社債市場全体の信用
環境は比較的安定しているが、AI関連の発行体だけが選別され、より高い上乗せ金利を求められ
ている。 
背景にあるのは、社債の供給量の多さだ。Alphabet、Amazon、Meta、Oracleの 4 社による社債
発行額は、10 月13 日時点で2,280 億ドルを超えた。これは2025 年の年間発行額の2 倍以上にあ
たる。2027 年にはハイパースケーラー全体の発行額が4,200 億ドルと、2026 年を6 割上回るとの
予想もある。投資対象が同業種の社債に偏ることを回避するため「AI以外の社債を求める」動き
も目立ち始めた。実際、9 月のAI関連の社債発行額は8 月の半分以下に落ち込んだ。 
こうした変化は、AI投資の資金調達に「量」と「価格」の両面から制約が生じ始めたことを示
している。巨額の手元資金を持つ大手ハイパースケーラーにとって、利回りの上昇は直ちに投資
を抑える要因にはならないだろう。一方、借入に頼ってデータセンターを建設するネオクラウド
企業は事情が異なる。資金調達コストの上昇は、事業計画の見直しにつながりやすい。AI投資の
「周辺部」から設備投資のペースが鈍れば、それは半導体需要の伸びの鈍化として波及していく
ことになる。

3.  韓国・台湾で出荷・在庫バランスに変調 
実際、半導体の需給にも変化の兆しが出ている。韓国の8 月の鉱工業生産統計で前年比をみる
と、半導体は出荷の伸びが＋2.5％にとどまる一方、在庫は＋8.8％増加した。出荷の伸びから在
庫の伸びを差し引いた出荷・在庫バランスは▲6.3 ポイントと、在庫の積み上がりが出荷を上回っ
ている。電子部品はさらに悪化が鮮明だ。出荷が前年比▲5.1％減少する一方、在庫は＋16.2％増

### Page 3

3/4 
加し、出荷・在庫バランスは▲21.3 ポイントまで落ち込んだ。台湾の電子部品の出荷・在庫バラ
ンスも▲13.0 ポイント（7 月）とマイナス圏に沈んでいる（図表2）。 
韓国の8 月の半導体輸出額は前年比3 倍を超え、過去最高を更新した。それにもかかわらず出
荷の伸びが2％台にとどまっていることは、輸出額の急増の多くが、メモリーをはじめとする価格
の上昇によってもたらされていることを示唆している。数量の面では、需要の勢いはすでに鈍り
始めている可能性がある。 
台湾でも同様の兆しがある。8 月の電子部品の生産指数は過去最高を更新したが、サーバー製品
の仕様変更や生産計画の調整を受け、伸びは政府の予想を下回った。より先行的な指標である9
月の製造業PMIを電子・光学産業についてみると、次のような変化が出ている。 
✓ 新規受注指数が10 ポイント以上低下 
✓ 受注残指数も10 ポイント以上下がり、54.3％と2025 年 12 月以来の低い水準になった 
✓ 在庫指数は64.8％へ上昇 
✓ 顧客在庫指数は51.0％と、2022 年12 月以来の高い水準で「過剰」に転じた 
一部の部品だけが不足し、ほかの部品が余るミスマッチも広がっている。そのため、受注と生
産がともに急拡大していた局面から、高水準ながら受注の勢いが鈍り、在庫の負担が増す局面へ
と移りつつある。 
この変調の背景には、AIの外側の需要が弱っていることもある。メモリー価格の急騰は、スマ
ートフォンやパソコンなど消費者向け電子機器のコストを押し上げている。スマートフォンなど
では値上げも相次いでいる。値上げによって買い替えの周期が長くなれば、AI以外の用途の半導
体や電子部品の需要が弱まり、在庫調整の動きが広がる可能性がある。

4.  それでも中長期のAI需要拡大は続く 
もっとも、こうした兆しをもって、AI関連需要の拡大が終わったと判断するのは早計だろう。 
※出荷在庫バランスは［出荷（前年比）－在庫（前年比）］
出所：Datastream
